    Amazon Rekognition to analyze the scanned images, Amazon Comprehend to detect entities and extract text from documents, and Amazon Kendra to provide natural language search capabilities: This option is incorrect.     Amazon Comprehend to categorize and index the documents, Amazon Lex to provide a conversational interface for querying the system, and Amazon S3 to store the extracted document metadata: This option is incorrect. Amazon Textract extracts text from scanned documents and PDFs, Amazon Comprehend analyzes the extracted text, and Amazon Kendra provides intelligent search across the document corpus.

Why Textract, Comprehend, and Kendra fit this scenario.

The source content is document-based.

The documents include scanned images and PDFs.

The solution must extract text, identify important entities, and support natural language search.

That points to Amazon Textract, Amazon Comprehend, and Amazon Kendra.

It can identify entities, key phrases, dates, organizations, project identifiers, and other domain-relevant terms.

This allows users to search using natural language instead of relying only on exact keyword matching.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Document OCR: Use Amazon Textract.

    Scanned images and PDFs: Extract text from document formats.

    Enterprise search: Use Amazon Kendra.

    Natural language queries: Let users search documents without exact keyword matching.

    Avoid service mismatch: Do not use Amazon Rekognition for document OCR or Amazon Lex for document search.

    Amazon Kendra: Provides intelligent enterprise search over document repositories.

Question 40Skipped

An ML engineer at StructaCore Engineering  a large construction and engineering firm  is using Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler to preprocess operational sensor and project management data for a machine learning initiative.


The engineer must import data from multiple enterprise sources, clean and transform the data, and engineer features suitable for model training  all while minimizing custom coding effort and leveraging Data Wrangler's built-in capabilities.


Which TWO of the following steps should the engineer take to prepare the data using Data Wrangler? (Select TWO.)


Correct selection
Import data from Amazon S3, Amazon Redshift, or SageMaker Feature Store directly into Data Wrangler as source connectors.
Explanation
Correct. Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler natively supports importing data from Amazon S3, Amazon Redshift, Amazon Athena, SageMaker Feature Store, and more than 40 additional enterprise sources through built-in connectors, requiring no custom ingestion code. This multi-source ingestion capability is a foundational feature that allows organizations with heterogeneous data architectures — such as StructaCore's sensor and project management systems — to consolidate datasets for preprocessing within a single visual workflow. Leveraging these connectors directly satisfies the requirement to minimize custom coding during the data import phase. Keyword tip: 'Import data into Data Wrangler from S3, Redshift, or SageMaker Feature Store using built-in connectors' is a canonical correct answer pattern in Data Wrangler source-ingestion questions.
Perform all data transformations using SageMaker Studio's standalone Jupyter notebooks, bypassing the Data Wrangler visual interface.
Explanation
Incorrect. Although SageMaker Studio Jupyter notebooks are a legitimate tool for custom ML development, using them as a wholesale replacement for Data Wrangler's visual interface defeats the purpose of adopting Data Wrangler in the first place. The service is specifically designed to simplify data preprocessing through an interactive guided UI, providing automatic code generation and pipeline integration without requiring notebook-based imperative code. Choosing notebooks over Data Wrangler's native flow adds unnecessary engineering complexity when low-code transformation is the stated goal. Keyword tip: In questions that highlight Data Wrangler's built-in transformation capabilities, options that substitute Jupyter notebooks as the primary transformation mechanism are distractors rather than complements.
Export the transformed data directly to Amazon Athena as the primary destination for downstream model training integration.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon Athena is a supported import source connector in Data Wrangler, but it is not a standard export destination for processed data intended for downstream model training. Data Wrangler exports transformed datasets to Amazon S3, SageMaker Pipelines, SageMaker Feature Store, or directly triggers SageMaker processing jobs and training jobs — none of which involve Athena as an output target. Conflating Athena's role as an import source with an export destination for training data is a common misconception that this distractor specifically exploits. Keyword tip: Distinguishing between Data Wrangler's supported import sources (S3, Redshift, Athena, Feature Store) and its valid export destinations (S3, SageMaker Pipelines, Feature Store, training jobs) is a reliable differentiator on exam questions about closing the Data Wrangler preprocessing loop.
Correct selection
Use Data Wrangler's built-in transform library to apply one-hot encoding on categorical variables as part of the feature engineering flow.
Explanation
Correct. SageMaker Data Wrangler includes a rich built-in feature engineering transform library that covers one-hot encoding for categorical variables, ordinal encoding, string indexing, binning, and many other transformations applicable without writing a single line of code. These transforms are applied interactively through the visual flow interface and are automatically translated into executable PySpark or pandas code when the flow is exported to a SageMaker Pipeline or processing job, ensuring full reproducibility. This directly satisfies both the feature engineering requirement and the low-code constraint stated in the scenario. Keyword tip: 'Built-in one-hot encoding,' 'categorical variable transformation,' and 'feature engineering flow' in Data Wrangler are reliable indicators of a correct answer in questions about pre-built transformation capabilities.
Manually write custom Python scripts outside of Data Wrangler to handle missing data imputation and feature normalization tasks.
Explanation
Incorrect. Writing custom Python scripts outside of Data Wrangler contradicts the core value proposition of the service and is unnecessary given the scenario's explicit requirement to minimize custom coding effort. Data Wrangler provides a visual, low-code interface with over 300 pre-built transformations — including missing value imputation and feature normalization — that eliminate the need for standalone scripting. Selecting this approach introduces maintenance overhead and forgoes the governed, reproducible preprocessing flows that Data Wrangler is designed to deliver. Keyword tip: When a question emphasizes Data Wrangler's built-in capabilities and minimal coding, any option that routes preprocessing through external custom scripts is a distractor.
Overall explanation

    Export the transformed data directly to Amazon Athena as the primary destination for downstream model training integration: This option is incorrect. Amazon Athena can be used as an import/query source, but it is not the primary export destination for transformed training data. Data Wrangler commonly exports to Amazon S3, Amazon SageMaker Pipelines, Amazon SageMaker Feature Store, or SageMaker processing/training workflows.

    Use Data Wrangler's built-in transform library to apply one-hot encoding on categorical variables as part of the feature engineering flow: This is a correct option. Amazon SageMaker Data Wrangler includes built-in feature engineering transforms such as one-hot encoding, allowing categorical variables to be converted into ML-ready numerical representations without custom code.

Why built-in connectors and one-hot encoding fit this scenario.

The team needs to import data from AWS data sources.

Question 41Skipped

An education technology company, LearnPath Analytics, is using Amazon SageMaker Debugger to monitor and debug a deep learning model during training.


The model is designed to predict student dropout risk based on engagement and assessment data. 


The team observes that the model's loss has plateaued and performance is no longer improving. They suspect the root cause is vanishing gradients propagating through the network.


Which of the following steps should the team take to diagnose and address this issue using SageMaker Debugger? 
Use Amazon SageMaker Neo to compile and optimize the model for target hardware, which will also resolve gradient instability during training.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Neo is a model compilation and optimization service designed to improve inference performance on specific target hardware, such as edge devices and cloud instances. It operates post-training and has no capability to influence gradient computation or training dynamics whatsoever. Keyword tip: SageMaker Neo = inference/deployment optimization; any option suggesting Neo resolves a training-time issue such as vanishing gradients is definitively incorrect.
Increase the batch size in the training script so that gradient aggregation across more samples reduces the vanishing gradient effect.
Explanation
Incorrect. Increasing batch size affects the variance and stability of gradient estimates across a mini-batch but does not fundamentally address vanishing gradients, which arise from the network architecture and the way gradients are computed during backpropagation through deep layers. Larger batches may smooth gradient noise but cannot correct the diminishing gradient magnitudes caused by deep layer depth or activation function saturation. Keyword tip: when 'batch size adjustment' is proposed as a fix for vanishing gradients, recognize it as a distractor targeting gradient noise rather than gradient magnitude — a common misdirection on training instability questions.
Correct answer
Set up a custom rule in SageMaker Debugger to detect vanishing gradients and adjust the learning rate or change the optimizer accordingly.
Explanation
Correct. Amazon SageMaker Debugger provides built-in rules (e.g., VanishingGradient) and supports custom rules authored using the smdebug library that monitor tensor values in real time during training. When vanishing gradients are detected, practitioners can respond by adjusting the learning rate (e.g., applying learning rate scheduling or warm-up), switching to gradient-friendly optimizers (e.g., Adam instead of vanilla SGD), applying gradient clipping, or modifying the network architecture (e.g., adding batch normalization or residual connections). This is the recommended MLOps pattern for diagnosing and correcting training instability using SageMaker Debugger. Keyword tip: SageMaker Debugger + VanishingGradient rule + optimizer or learning rate adjustment = canonical answer for gradient-related training instability questions on the MLA-C01 exam.
Manually stop the training job, augment the dataset with additional training samples, and restart the job to resolve the vanishing gradient problem.
Explanation
Incorrect. Adding more training data addresses data insufficiency problems such as underfitting or poor generalization, but it does not resolve vanishing gradients. Vanishing gradients are caused by the mathematical properties of activation functions and network depth during backpropagation, not by dataset size. Restarting the job without changing the model architecture, optimizer, or learning rate would reproduce the identical gradient issue. Keyword tip: 'more training data' is a distractor when the scenario clearly describes a numerical or optimization problem — not a data insufficiency or distribution problem.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of Amazon SageMaker Debugger and its ability to monitor training internals.

It focuses on detecting gradient-related training issues, such as vanishing gradients, and selecting the right training-time remediation strategy.

It is tied to the ML Model Development domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Increase the batch size in the training script so that gradient aggregation across more samples reduces the vanishing gradient effect: This option is incorrect. Increasing batch size can affect gradient estimate stability, but it does not directly solve vanishing gradients. Vanishing gradients are usually caused by deep network architecture, activation functions, and backpropagation behavior.

    Use Amazon SageMaker Neo to compile and optimize the model for target hardware, which will also resolve gradient instability during training: This option is incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Neo optimizes trained models for inference on target hardware. It does not affect training-time gradient computation or resolve vanishing gradients.

    Set up a custom rule in SageMaker Debugger to detect vanishing gradients and adjust the learning rate or change the optimizer accordingly: This is the correct option. Amazon SageMaker Debugger can monitor tensors such as gradients, weights, and activations during training. It supports built-in and custom rules for detecting issues such as vanishing gradients. Once detected, the team can adjust the learning rate, optimizer, or training strategy.

    Manually stop the training job, augment the dataset with additional training samples, and restart the job to resolve the vanishing gradient problem: This option is incorrect. More data can help with generalization, but it does not directly fix vanishing gradients. The issue is related to training dynamics and gradient flow, not dataset size.

Why SageMaker Debugger with a vanishing gradient rule fits this scenario.

The scenario describes training instability.

The specific issue is vanishing gradients.

That means gradients become extremely small as they propagate backward through the network.

When this happens, earlier layers learn very slowly or stop learning effectively.

That points to Amazon SageMaker Debugger.

Amazon SageMaker Debugger can capture tensors during training.

These tensors can include weights, gradients, activations, and losses.

The service supports built-in rules and custom rules for detecting problematic training behavior.

For vanishing gradients, the team can use a built-in rule such as VanishingGradient or create a custom rule with smdebug.

After the issue is detected, the correct remediation is training-time adjustment.

Examples include changing the optimizer, tuning the learning rate, using learning rate scheduling, applying gradient clipping, or modifying architecture choices such as activation functions, normalization layers, or residual connections.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Detect training instability: Use Amazon SageMaker Debugger.

    Monitor gradients: Capture tensor data during training.

    Identify vanishing gradients: Use a built-in or custom rule.

    Apply training-time remediation: Adjust learning rate, optimizer, or architecture.

    Avoid inference-tool confusion: Do not use Amazon SageMaker Neo.

    Avoid data-volume confusion: Do not solve gradient flow issues by simply adding more data.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Vanishing gradients + training instability + tensor monitoring = Amazon SageMaker Debugger.

Remember:

    Amazon SageMaker Debugger: Monitors training internals such as tensors and system metrics.

    VanishingGradient rule: Detects gradients that become too small.

    smdebug: Library used for custom debugging rules and tensor analysis.

    Learning rate adjustment: Valid remediation for training instability.

    Optimizer change: Valid remediation when gradient behavior is poor.

    Amazon SageMaker Neo: Inference optimization, not training debugging.

    Data augmentation: Helps data coverage, not vanishing-gradient mechanics.

Official AWS references.

    Use Amazon SageMaker Debugger.

    Amazon SageMaker Debugger built-in rules.

Key exam terms: Amazon SageMaker Debugger, VanishingGradient rule, smdebug, custom rule, training instability, optimizer adjustment, learning rate scheduling, gradient clipping.

Question 42Skipped

A semiconductor manufacturer, SiliconEdge Analytics, uses Amazon SageMaker to train machine learning models for predictive yield optimization.


The team stores raw sensor telemetry data  such as wafer inspection events and fabrication process interactions  in an Amazon S3 bucket.


They need to preprocess this data to remove duplicates, normalize numerical features, and encode categorical variables before model training. The preprocessing solution must scale automatically to accommodate continuously increasing data volumes and integrate natively with the SageMaker training workflow.


Which solution will most effectively meet these requirements? 
Use AWS Glue ETL jobs to preprocess the data, apply deduplication, normalization, and encoding transforms, and then load the transformed data into Amazon SageMaker for training.
Explanation
Incorrect. AWS Glue is a capable serverless ETL service and can handle deduplication and basic transformations at scale. However, it is optimized for general-purpose data engineering pipelines rather than ML-specific preprocessing workflows. AWS Glue lacks native ML preprocessing primitives (e.g., Scikit-learn feature transformers), and integrating it into a SageMaker training workflow requires additional orchestration steps that introduce unnecessary complexity compared to SageMaker Processing Jobs. Keyword tip: AWS Glue is the right answer when the scenario emphasizes data catalog management, cross-source ETL, or Spark-based data engineering — not ML-specific feature engineering tightly coupled to SageMaker.
Correct answer
Use Amazon SageMaker Processing Jobs with a pre-built container for data preprocessing, and store the processed data back in Amazon S3.
Explanation
Correct. Amazon SageMaker Processing Jobs provide a fully managed, scalable environment specifically designed for ML preprocessing tasks such as deduplication, feature normalization, and categorical encoding. They support pre-built containers (Scikit-learn, Spark, and custom Docker images), integrate natively with SageMaker Pipelines and training jobs, and scale automatically without requiring infrastructure management. This is the canonical MLOps pattern for data preparation within a SageMaker-centric ML workflow, making it the ideal fit for SiliconEdge Analytics' growing sensor telemetry volumes. Keyword tip: when the scenario requires scalable, SageMaker-integrated preprocessing with minimal operational overhead, SageMaker Processing Jobs is the preferred choice on MLA-C01.
Write a preprocessing script in Python and run it on an Amazon EMR cluster with auto-scaling enabled, storing the output in Amazon S3 before launching a SageMaker training job.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon EMR with auto-scaling can handle large-scale distributed preprocessing using Spark or Hadoop, making it technically capable for this use case. However, managing an EMR cluster introduces significant operational overhead — cluster configuration, bootstrapping, scaling policies, and cost management — that is not justified when SageMaker Processing Jobs already provide a managed, auto-scaling environment with native SageMaker integration. EMR is preferred when preprocessing logic requires deep Spark customization or when the job is part of a broader existing Hadoop ecosystem. Keyword tip: choose EMR over SageMaker Processing Jobs only when the scenario explicitly involves complex distributed Spark workloads, pre-existing EMR infrastructure, or processing requirements that exceed what managed SageMaker containers support.
Use Amazon Athena to query the raw data and perform all preprocessing transformations using SQL, then export the processed dataset back to Amazon S3 for training.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon Athena is an interactive SQL query service optimized for ad hoc analysis of data stored in Amazon S3 using standard SQL. While Athena can perform some data filtering and basic transformations via SQL, it does not support ML-specific preprocessing operations such as numerical normalization, one-hot encoding, or scalable feature engineering pipelines, and it is not designed to replace a full preprocessing stage in an ML workflow. Keyword tip: Athena is the right answer when the scenario involves querying, exploration, or lightweight SQL-based data filtering — not when ML feature engineering or scalable transformation integrated with a SageMaker training workflow is required.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests the ability to select the appropriate AWS service for scalable, ML-integrated data preprocessing inside a Amazon SageMaker workflow.

It focuses on choosing Amazon SageMaker Processing Jobs when the requirement is to run preprocessing code in a managed environment and store the processed output in Amazon S3 for downstream training.

It is tied to the Data Preparation for Machine Learning (ML) domain.

    Use Amazon SageMaker Processing Jobs with a pre-built container for data preprocessing, and store the processed data back in Amazon S3: This is the correct option. They support pre-built containers and custom containers, and they integrate naturally with Amazon SageMaker training workflows.

    Use AWS Glue ETL jobs to preprocess the data, apply deduplication, normalization, and encoding transforms, and then load the transformed data into Amazon SageMaker for training: This option is possible, but not the best fit. AWS Glue is a general-purpose ETL service. It is useful for data engineering and catalog-driven pipelines, but it is not as directly integrated into Amazon SageMaker ML preprocessing workflows as Amazon SageMaker Processing Jobs.

    Write a preprocessing script in Python and run it on an Amazon EMR cluster with auto-scaling enabled, storing the output in Amazon S3 before launching a SageMaker training job: This option is technically feasible, but it adds unnecessary operational overhead. Amazon EMR is powerful for distributed Spark or Hadoop workloads, but cluster provisioning, scaling, and maintenance are not required when Amazon SageMaker Processing Jobs can run the preprocessing workload natively.

    Use Amazon Athena to query the raw data and perform all preprocessing transformations using SQL, then export the processed dataset back to Amazon S3 for training: This option is incorrect. Amazon Athena is useful for querying data in Amazon S3 with SQL, but it is not a full ML preprocessing environment. It is not ideal for feature normalization, categorical encoding, or reusable ML preprocessing logic inside a SageMaker-centric pipeline.

Why SageMaker Processing Jobs fit this scenario.

The scenario requires scalable preprocessing.

The output must be prepared for model training.

The workflow is centered on Amazon SageMaker.

That points directly to Amazon SageMaker Processing Jobs.

They can use pre-built containers, such as containers for Scikit-learn or Spark, or custom containers when the team needs more control.

Common preprocessing tasks include deduplication, data validation, normalization, categorical encoding, train/test splitting, feature transformation, and data quality preparation.

Processed data can be written back to Amazon S3.

That output can then be consumed by Amazon SageMaker training jobs or orchestrated through Amazon SageMaker Pipelines.

This avoids managing an Amazon EMR cluster and avoids stitching together a separate ETL service when the preprocessing workload belongs directly inside the ML lifecycle.

    Native SageMaker integration: Connect preprocessing to training and pipeline orchestration.

Question 43Skipped

A global hotel chain, StayBridge Resorts, is building a multi-lingual guest support system that allows guests to communicate both via text and voice.


The system must transcribe inbound guest support calls into text, translate that text into each guest's preferred language, and generate audio responses in the same target language — all within a single integrated pipeline.


Which combination of AWS services is MOST appropriate for this use case?
Correct answer
Use Amazon Transcribe to convert speech to text, Amazon Translate to translate the text, and Amazon Polly to convert the translated text to speech.
Explanation
Correct. This combination maps each pipeline stage to the purpose-built AWS AI service designed for it: Amazon Transcribe performs automatic speech recognition (ASR) to convert guest audio input into text; Amazon Translate applies neural machine translation to convert the text into the guest's preferred language; and Amazon Polly uses text-to-speech (TTS) synthesis to generate a natural-sounding audio response in the target language. Together, these three fully managed services form a scalable, serverless multi-lingual voice pipeline without requiring any custom ML model development or operational overhead. Keyword tip: whenever an MLA-C01 question requires an end-to-end speech-in / speech-out pipeline with language translation, the canonical answer is Transcribe → Translate → Polly.
Use Amazon Transcribe to convert speech to text, Amazon Lex to handle translations, and Amazon Polly to generate the voice responses.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon Lex is a managed service for building conversational interfaces using intent recognition, slot filling, and dialog management — it does not perform language translation between different human languages. The correct service for neural machine translation in an AWS pipeline is Amazon Translate. Substituting Lex for Translate would leave the translation requirement entirely unaddressed, as Lex operates on recognized intents within a single language, not cross-language text conversion. Keyword tip: Amazon Lex handles dialog flow and natural language understanding (NLU), not cross-language translation; always distinguish Lex (conversational AI) from Translate (language conversion) when evaluating multi-lingual pipeline architectures.
Use Amazon Polly to convert speech to text, Amazon Translate to handle text translations, and Amazon Transcribe to generate the audio responses.
Explanation
Incorrect. This option reverses the roles of Amazon Polly and Amazon Transcribe. Amazon Polly is a text-to-speech service that synthesizes spoken audio from written text — it cannot transcribe or recognize speech. Amazon Transcribe is the automatic speech recognition service that converts audio to text, not the other way around. Neither service supports the inverted function described, making this combination technically invalid for both the ASR and TTS stages of the pipeline. Keyword tip: a reliable MLA-C01 pattern to memorize — Transcribe = speech-to-text (ASR), Polly = text-to-speech (TTS); these roles are never interchangeable regardless of the scenario context.
Use Amazon Transcribe to convert speech to text, Amazon Translate to handle text translations, and Amazon SageMaker to generate speech responses.
Explanation
Incorrect. While Amazon Transcribe is correctly selected for speech-to-text and Amazon Translate for text translation, Amazon SageMaker is a managed ML platform used for training, tuning, and deploying custom models — it is not a purpose-built speech synthesis service. Generating audio responses with SageMaker would require building, hosting, and maintaining a custom TTS model, introducing unnecessary complexity, cost, and operational overhead when Amazon Polly already provides fully managed, production-ready TTS functionality. Keyword tip: avoid selecting SageMaker when a purpose-built AWS AI service such as Polly for TTS already satisfies the requirement; SageMaker is for custom model development, not a substitute for managed AI services.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of the two-part setup required to activate Amazon SageMaker Profiler for CPU and GPU monitoring in a PyTorch training job.

It focuses on configuring profiling at both levels:

    The Amazon SageMaker Estimator configuration.

    The training script instrumentation.

It is tied to the Deployment and Orchestration of ML Workflows domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Manually enable automatic SageMaker Profiler optimization in the SageMaker console to reduce memory overhead during training: This option is incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Profiler is not enabled through a one-click console optimization setting. It requires explicit programmatic configuration. It also observes resource usage; it does not automatically optimize memory overhead.

    Define the profiler configuration in your SageMaker Estimator, specifying the duration of the profiling in seconds for the CPU and GPU: This is a correct option. The Amazon SageMaker Estimator must include a ProfilerConfig through the profiler_config parameter. This defines profiling behavior, such as the output location and system monitoring interval.

    Use SageMaker Neo to compile the model for optimized CPU and GPU performance before the training job begins: This option is incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Neo compiles trained models for optimized inference on target hardware. It is not used to profile CPU and GPU utilization during training.

    Configure SageMaker Profiler to automatically tune hyperparameters such as learning rate based on observed resource utilization data: This option is incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Profiler is an observability and diagnostics tool. It does not tune hyperparameters. Hyperparameter optimization is handled by Amazon SageMaker Automatic Model Tuning.

    Import the necessary SageMaker Profiler modules and add start and stop profiling commands in the PyTorch training script: This is a correct option. The training script must import the required profiler modules and use profiling hooks such as start_profiling() and stop_profiling() to define the profiling window.

Why ProfilerConfig and script instrumentation fit this scenario.

The scenario requires CPU and GPU monitoring during a PyTorch training job.

That requires configuration in two places.

First, the Amazon SageMaker Estimator must define the profiler configuration.

This is done with ProfilerConfig.

The profiler configuration tells Amazon SageMaker where to store profiling data and how frequently to collect system metrics.

Second, the training script must be instrumented.

For a PyTorch job, the script needs the required Amazon SageMaker Profiler imports and profiling hooks.

The start_profiling() and stop_profiling() calls define the part of the training script that should be profiled.

Together, these two pieces allow Amazon SageMaker Profiler to collect system resource usage and framework-level training information.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Estimator-level setup: Configure ProfilerConfig.

    Training script setup: Add profiler imports and start/stop hooks.

    CPU and GPU visibility: Capture system resource usage.

    PyTorch profiling: Instrument the framework training script.

    S3 output storage: Store profiling artifacts in Amazon S3.

    Avoid service confusion: Do not use Amazon SageMaker Neo or Amazon SageMaker Automatic Model Tuning for training profiling.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

SageMaker Profiler + PyTorch training job = ProfilerConfig in the Estimator + start/stop profiling hooks in the script.

Remember:

    Amazon SageMaker Profiler: Observes CPU, GPU, memory, and framework-level training behavior.

    ProfilerConfig: Estimator-level configuration for profiling.

    profiler_config: Estimator parameter used to enable profiler settings.

    start_profiling() and stop_profiling(): Script-level hooks that define the profiling window.

    Amazon SageMaker Neo: Compiles trained models for inference optimization.

    Amazon SageMaker Automatic Model Tuning: Tunes hyperparameters, not profiling.

    Profiler role: Diagnostics and observability, not automatic optimization.

Question 44Skipped

A data analyst at PrecisionForge Industries, an industrial manufacturing company, wants to extract meaningful insights from unstructured customer feedback stored in Amazon S3.


The insights must include the identification of key entities such as people, organizations, and locations mentioned in the feedback, as well as a determination of the overall sentiment expressed in each piece of feedback. Which combination of Amazon Comprehend features should the analyst use to achieve this goal?
Correct answer
Entity Recognition and Sentiment Analysis
Explanation
Correct. Amazon Comprehend's Entity Recognition (DetectEntities API) identifies and classifies named entities — including PERSON, ORGANIZATION, LOCATION, DATE, QUANTITY, and more — directly from raw unstructured text. Its Sentiment Analysis (DetectSentiment API) returns a sentiment label (POSITIVE, NEGATIVE, NEUTRAL, or MIXED) along with confidence scores for each individual document. Together, these two features precisely address both requirements stated in the scenario: structured typed-entity extraction and overall sentiment determination per feedback record. Keyword tip: whenever a scenario requires typed entity extraction (people, organizations, locations) combined with sentiment scoring from unstructured text, the canonical Amazon Comprehend answer is Entity Recognition paired with Sentiment Analysis.
Text Classification and Entity Recognition
Explanation
Incorrect. Text Classification in Amazon Comprehend assigns documents to predefined custom or built-in categories, but it does not extract named entities such as people, organizations, or locations. It also produces no sentiment score. Because neither the entity-extraction requirement nor the sentiment requirement is satisfied by Text Classification alone, pairing it with Entity Recognition still leaves the sentiment requirement unmet. Keyword tip: when a question simultaneously requires named-entity extraction and sentiment determination, Text Classification is a reliable distractor because it addresses neither task.
Keyphrase Extraction and Sentiment Analysis
Explanation
Incorrect. Keyphrase Extraction identifies important noun phrases and terms within a document, which can surface relevant concepts, but it does not classify text into typed entity categories such as PERSON, ORGANIZATION, or LOCATION — that is the exclusive role of Entity Recognition. Although Sentiment Analysis is correctly included in this option and satisfies the sentiment requirement, the absence of Entity Recognition means the named-entity requirement remains unmet. Keyword tip: Keyphrase Extraction and Entity Recognition are distinct Amazon Comprehend features; statistically significant phrases are not equivalent to typed named entities.
Topic Modeling and Keyphrase Extraction
Explanation
Incorrect. Topic Modeling in Amazon Comprehend uses a Latent Dirichlet Allocation-based asynchronous job to discover abstract thematic clusters across an entire document corpus, not within individual records. Keyphrase Extraction identifies statistically significant noun phrases within a single document but does not classify text into typed entity categories such as PERSON, ORGANIZATION, or LOCATION, nor does it produce a sentiment score. This pairing satisfies neither of the two stated requirements. Keyword tip: Topic Modeling operates at the corpus level and is incompatible with per-document entity typing or sentiment scoring requirements.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests the ability to map specific NLP requirements to the correct Amazon Comprehend features.

It focuses on selecting Entity Recognition when the requirement is to extract named entities, and Sentiment Analysis when the requirement is to determine the emotional tone of text.

It is tied to the Data Preparation for Machine Learning (ML) domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Text Classification and Entity Recognition: This option is incorrect. Text Classification assigns documents to categories, but it does not provide sentiment scoring. Although Entity Recognition satisfies the named-entity extraction requirement, the sentiment requirement remains unmet.

    Topic Modeling and Keyphrase Extraction: This option is incorrect. Topic Modeling discovers themes across a document corpus, and Keyphrase Extraction identifies important phrases. Neither feature extracts typed named entities and neither returns document sentiment.

    Keyphrase Extraction and Sentiment Analysis: This option is partially correct but still incorrect overall. Sentiment Analysis satisfies the sentiment requirement, but Keyphrase Extraction does not identify typed named entities such as people, organizations, or locations.

    Entity Recognition and Sentiment Analysis: This is the correct option. Entity Recognition extracts and classifies named entities from unstructured text. Sentiment Analysis determines whether the text is positive, negative, neutral, or mixed.

Why Entity Recognition and Sentiment Analysis fit this scenario.

The scenario has two requirements.

First, the system must extract named entities from unstructured text.

That points to Amazon Comprehend Entity Recognition.

Entity Recognition can identify entity types such as PERSON, ORGANIZATION, LOCATION, DATE, QUANTITY, and other supported entity categories.

Second, the system must determine the sentiment of the text.

That points to Amazon Comprehend Sentiment Analysis.

Sentiment Analysis returns labels such as POSITIVE, NEGATIVE, NEUTRAL, or MIXED, along with confidence scores.

Together, these two features satisfy both requirements.

Keyphrase Extraction is not enough because key phrases are not the same as typed named entities.

Text Classification is not enough because it assigns categories but does not provide sentiment.

Topic Modeling is not enough because it discovers themes across a corpus rather than extracting named entities and sentiment per document.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Named entity extraction: Use Entity Recognition.

    Typed entity output: Identify people, organizations, locations, dates, and quantities.

    Sentiment detection: Use Sentiment Analysis.

    Unstructured text processing: Use Amazon Comprehend.

    Avoid keyphrase confusion: Do not treat key phrases as named entities.

    Avoid classification confusion: Do not use Text Classification when sentiment scoring is required.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Named entities + sentiment from unstructured text = Amazon Comprehend Entity Recognition + Sentiment Analysis.

Remember:

    Entity Recognition: Extracts typed named entities.

    Sentiment Analysis: Returns sentiment labels and confidence scores.

    DetectEntities: API used for entity detection.

    DetectSentiment: API used for sentiment detection.

    Keyphrase Extraction: Finds important phrases, not typed entities.

    Text Classification: Assigns categories, not sentiment.

    Topic Modeling: Finds corpus-level themes, not per-document sentiment.

Official AWS references.

    How Amazon Comprehend works.

Key exam terms: Amazon Comprehend, Entity Recognition, Sentiment Analysis, DetectEntities API, DetectSentiment API, named entities, unstructured text, Keyphrase Extraction, Text Classification, Topic Modeling.

Question 45Skipped

A machine learning engineer at AeroVantage Systems, an aerospace and defense contractor, is setting up an isolated development environment to iteratively build, train, and deploy machine learning models using Amazon SageMaker.


The engineer requires a fully managed Jupyter notebook environment that eliminates manual infrastructure provisioning, comes with popular ML libraries such as TensorFlow and PyTorch pre-installed, and automatically persists all work to durable storage so that sessions can be resumed at any time without data loss. Which Amazon SageMaker feature BEST meets all of these requirements?



SageMaker Studio with JupyterLab spaces configured on a shared domain
Explanation
Incorrect. SageMaker Studio with JupyterLab spaces does provide a managed Jupyter environment with pre-installed ML libraries and persistent storage. However, Studio is architected as a broader, multi-user, collaborative MLOps platform that requires domain and user profile configuration, shared Amazon EFS-backed storage, and additional administrative setup — overhead that is unnecessary for a single engineer seeking a self-contained, minimal-configuration notebook environment. SageMaker Notebook Instances are the purpose-built, dedicated solution for that specific use case. Keyword tip: when the scenario emphasizes a 'fully managed individual notebook with minimal administrative setup' for a single user, prefer SageMaker Notebook Instances over SageMaker Studio.
Correct answer
SageMaker Notebook Instances
Explanation
Correct. SageMaker Notebook Instances are fully managed ML compute instances running Jupyter that handle all underlying infrastructure provisioning, including server configuration, security patching, and network setup. They ship with pre-installed ML frameworks such as TensorFlow, PyTorch, MXNet, and Scikit-learn via AWS-maintained conda environments. Each instance is backed by a persistent Amazon EBS volume, which automatically saves notebooks and associated files so that work is retained across session starts and stops. This directly satisfies all three stated requirements: managed infrastructure, pre-installed libraries, and automatic persistence. Keyword tip: 'fully managed Jupyter + pre-installed ML libraries + persistent EBS-backed storage on a single ML compute instance' maps directly to SageMaker Notebook Instances.
Amazon EC2 instance with a Deep Learning AMI and Jupyter notebook manually launched
Explanation
Incorrect. While Amazon EC2 instances launched with a Deep Learning AMI include pre-installed ML frameworks and can run Jupyter notebooks, they require the engineer to manually start the Jupyter server, configure security groups for port access, manage software updates, and handle instance lifecycle operations. This is not a fully managed experience and introduces significant operational overhead that is absent from SageMaker Notebook Instances. The Deep Learning AMI addresses the library requirement but fails the 'fully managed' and 'no manual provisioning' constraints. Keyword tip: any option requiring manual launch or configuration of Jupyter on Amazon EC2 is eliminated immediately when 'fully managed' and 'no infrastructure provisioning' are explicit requirements.
SageMaker Training Jobs with a custom script entry point and interactive debugging enabled via SageMaker Debugger
Explanation
Incorrect. SageMaker Training Jobs with SageMaker Debugger provide managed, scalable training compute with real-time tensor inspection and debugging hooks, but they are designed for executing discrete training workloads in isolated containers — not for interactive, iterative development within a persistent Jupyter notebook interface. No live Jupyter environment is exposed to the engineer during a training job run. This option conflates scalable model training execution with interactive notebook-based development. Keyword tip: SageMaker Training Jobs with Debugger address scalable, reproducible training runs and tensor-level introspection, not interactive notebook-based iterative development workflows.
Overall explanation

Question 46Skipped

A platform engineering team at a healthcare diagnostics provider, MedCore Diagnostics, needs to monitor the CPU and memory utilization of containerized inference services running in Amazon ECS.


In addition, they must capture custom operational metrics such as the number of active inference requests per second.


Which combination of AWS services and features is the MOST appropriate to satisfy both the standard and custom monitoring requirements with minimal operational overhead?
Enable AWS Config to track ECS task performance changes and set up Amazon CloudWatch Alarms for CPU and memory metric thresholds.
Explanation
Incorrect. AWS Config is a configuration compliance and change-tracking service that records changes to the configuration state of AWS resources — such as ECS task definitions or security group rules — but it does not collect or expose runtime performance metrics such as CPU utilization, memory pressure, or application-level counters. Although Amazon CloudWatch Alarms can fire on metric thresholds, AWS Config cannot act as the source of those runtime metrics. Keyword tip: AWS Config answers 'was this resource compliant with policy?', not 'how is this resource performing at runtime?' — a critical distinction when evaluating monitoring architecture options on the MLA-C01 exam.
Configure AWS CloudTrail to capture ECS API calls and use Amazon EventBridge rules to forward CPU and memory metrics into Amazon CloudWatch.
Explanation
Incorrect. AWS CloudTrail is a governance and audit service that records API calls made to AWS services; it does not capture runtime performance metrics such as CPU or memory utilization of ECS tasks. Amazon EventBridge cannot derive or forward performance metrics that CloudTrail never collected in the first place. Keyword tip: CloudTrail tracks 'who did what and when' (control-plane events), not 'how much CPU or memory a task is consuming' (data-plane metrics) — confusing these two services is a common MLA-C01 distractor pattern.
Use AWS Lambda to regularly poll ECS task performance data and push CPU and memory metrics into Amazon CloudWatch for analysis.
Explanation
Incorrect. Using AWS Lambda to periodically poll ECS task performance data and push metrics to Amazon CloudWatch introduces unnecessary operational complexity and latency. Amazon CloudWatch natively integrates with Amazon ECS to collect CPU and memory utilization metrics automatically without requiring a custom polling solution. Keyword tip: when you see 'Lambda polling for metrics,' recognize this as an anti-pattern when native CloudWatch–ECS integration already provides the same capability at lower cost and operational overhead.
Correct answer
Use Amazon CloudWatch to monitor ECS task-level metrics such as CPU and memory utilization natively. Install the CloudWatch Agent on the ECS tasks to push custom metrics, such as the number of active inference requests per second, into CloudWatch.
Explanation
Correct. Amazon CloudWatch natively collects ECS task-level metrics — including CPUUtilization and MemoryUtilization — at both the cluster and task level without any manual instrumentation. For application-defined custom metrics such as the number of active inference requests per second, the CloudWatch Agent can be deployed as a sidecar container or daemon service within ECS to emit those metrics directly to CloudWatch using the PutMetricData API. This approach consolidates both infrastructure and application-level monitoring in a single service with minimal operational overhead, which is the AWS-recommended pattern for comprehensive ECS observability. Keyword tip: 'native ECS metrics combined with the CloudWatch Agent for custom application metrics' is the canonical low-overhead monitoring pattern for containerized inference workloads on Amazon ECS.
Overall explanation

Question 47Skipped

A data scientist at NovaSoft Systems, a mid-size enterprise software vendor, is building a machine learning pipeline using Amazon SageMaker Pipelines. The pipeline includes data preprocessing, model training, and model registration steps.


The data scientist needs to ensure that hyperparameters and configuration values used during model training can be easily adjusted at execution time without modifying the pipeline definition itself, supporting rapid experimentation across multiple runs.


Which feature of SageMaker Pipelines should the data scientist use to achieve this requirement? 
Directed Acyclic Graph (DAG)
Explanation
Incorrect. The Directed Acyclic Graph (DAG) in SageMaker Pipelines defines the execution order and dependencies between steps, ensuring that each step runs only after its upstream dependencies have completed successfully. It governs execution topology and does not provide any mechanism for injecting or overriding configuration values at runtime without modifying the pipeline definition. Keyword tip: When a scenario focuses on controlling execution order or step dependencies rather than runtime configurability, DAG is the relevant concept — not Pipeline Parameters.
SageMaker Experiments
Explanation
Incorrect. SageMaker Experiments is a tracking and organization capability that records metrics, parameters, and artifacts associated with training runs, enabling data scientists to compare outcomes across iterations. While it complements iterative ML development workflows, it operates as an observability layer and does not provide a mechanism for declaring overridable variables that control pipeline execution behavior at runtime. Keyword tip: When a scenario emphasizes logging, comparing, or auditing training runs rather than controlling what values are passed into a pipeline at execution time, SageMaker Experiments is the relevant service.
Pipeline Steps
Explanation
Incorrect. Pipeline Steps define the individual units of work within a SageMaker Pipeline, such as a ProcessingStep, TrainingStep, or RegisterModelStep, each encapsulating a specific workload and its associated resources. While steps can consume declared parameters as inputs, they are not themselves the mechanism for declaring externally overridable variables — hardcoding values directly inside a step still requires modifying the pipeline definition to change behavior across runs. Keyword tip: When a question distinguishes between the container of work versus the configuration injected into that work, steps represent the former and Pipeline Parameters represent the latter.
Correct answer
Pipeline Parameters
Explanation
Correct. Pipeline Parameters in SageMaker Pipelines allow data scientists to declare named, typed variables — such as ParameterInteger, ParameterFloat, and ParameterString — with optional default values at pipeline definition time. These parameters can be overridden each time a pipeline execution is triggered via the AWS SDK, AWS CLI, or the SageMaker console, enabling different hyperparameter sets, dataset paths, or instance types to be supplied across runs without any modification to the underlying pipeline definition. This decoupling of configuration from logic is the canonical MLOps pattern for supporting rapid experimentation on a shared, reusable pipeline. Keyword tip: The phrase 'adjust at execution time without modifying the pipeline definition' is the direct signal for Pipeline Parameters on the MLA-C01 exam.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of Amazon SageMaker Pipelines parameterization.

It focuses on using Pipeline Parameters when the same pipeline definition must support different runtime values, such as hyperparameters, dataset paths, or instance types, without redefining the pipeline.

It is tied to the Deployment and Orchestration of ML Workflows domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Directed Acyclic Graph: This option is incorrect. The DAG defines the execution order and dependencies between pipeline steps. It controls workflow structure, but it does not provide runtime configuration injection.

    Pipeline Steps: This option is incorrect. Pipeline Steps define individual units of work, such as ProcessingStep, TrainingStep, or RegisterModelStep. Steps can consume parameters, but they are not the mechanism for declaring runtime-overridable values.

    Pipeline Parameters: This is the correct option. Pipeline Parameters let teams define named, typed variables in a Amazon SageMaker Pipeline. These values can be overridden when the pipeline execution starts, allowing reuse of the same pipeline definition across multiple runs.

    SageMaker Experiments: This option is incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Experiments tracks and compares training runs, metrics, parameters, and artifacts. It supports experiment observability, but it does not inject runtime configuration into a pipeline.

Why Pipeline Parameters fit this scenario.

The scenario requires flexibility at execution time.

The team wants to reuse the same pipeline definition.

They need to change values such as dataset paths, hyperparameters, or instance types without editing the pipeline logic.

That points directly to Pipeline Parameters.

In Amazon SageMaker Pipelines, parameters are declared when the pipeline is defined.

Examples include ParameterString, ParameterInteger, and ParameterFloat.

These parameters can have default values.

When a new pipeline execution starts, the team can override those values through the AWS SDK, AWS CLI, or the Amazon SageMaker console.

This separates pipeline logic from runtime configuration.

The same compiled pipeline can support multiple experiments, environments, datasets, and training configurations.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Runtime flexibility: Use Pipeline Parameters.

    Reusable pipeline definition: Avoid redefining the pipeline for each run.

    Configurable training values: Pass hyperparameters, dataset paths, or instance types at execution time.

    Typed variables: Use ParameterString, ParameterInteger, or ParameterFloat.

    MLOps parameterization: Decouple configuration from orchestration logic.

    Avoid observability confusion: Do not use Amazon SageMaker Experiments for runtime configuration injection.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Change values at execution time without modifying the pipeline definition = Pipeline Parameters.

Remember:

    Pipeline Parameters: Runtime-overridable variables.

    ParameterString: String input value for a pipeline execution.

    ParameterInteger: Integer input value for a pipeline execution.

    ParameterFloat: Floating-point input value for a pipeline execution.

    DAG: Defines dependency and execution order.

    Pipeline Steps: Define units of work.

    Amazon SageMaker Experiments: Tracks and compares runs, not pipeline configuration injection.

Official AWS references.

    Build and manage parameters in Amazon SageMaker Pipelines.

Key exam terms: Pipeline Parameters, Amazon SageMaker Pipelines, ParameterInteger, ParameterString, runtime configuration, pipeline redefinition, MLOps parameterization, rapid experimentation.

Question 48Skipped

A regional banking institution, NorthBridge Financial, uses an AWS Lambda function triggered by Amazon SNS to preprocess customer transaction data before sending it to an Amazon SageMaker model for fraud detection.

The transaction data is subsequently stored in Amazon RDS for long-term analytics.


The bank requires that only valid transactions (based on a predefined schema) are passed to the SageMaker model, and that invalid transactions are logged and stored in Amazon S3 for further analysis  without degrading the performance or throughput of the fraud detection pipeline.


Which of the following solutions BEST fits the bank's requirements?


Set up an AWS Step Functions workflow to orchestrate schema validation, invoke AWS Lambda for processing valid transactions and sending them to SageMaker, and write invalid transactions to Amazon S3.
Explanation
Incorrect. While AWS Step Functions can orchestrate multi-step workflows with complex branching, retry strategies, and long-running processes, introducing a full state machine solely for schema validation and S3 writes in an SNS-triggered, per-transaction pipeline adds unnecessary operational overhead and latency. Step Functions is best justified when workflows require coordination across multiple services with conditional branching or human approval steps. For this straightforward two-path routing scenario — valid transactions to SageMaker and invalid transactions to S3 — the SQS-decoupled Lambda pattern is more appropriate and cost-effective. Keyword tip: When a scenario involves only two execution paths with simple branching at transaction-level granularity, AWS Step Functions introduces disproportionate overhead compared to an SQS-triggered Lambda pattern.
Correct answer
Use AWS Lambda to validate the schema of each transaction, send valid data to SageMaker for real-time inference, and forward invalid data to an Amazon SQS queue, which triggers a separate Lambda function to log and store it in Amazon S3.
Explanation
Correct. This architecture cleanly decouples schema validation from invalid-data handling, preserving low latency on the critical fraud detection path. The primary Lambda function focuses on validating the transaction schema and invoking the SageMaker real-time endpoint, ensuring throughput is not compromised by I/O operations for invalid records. Invalid transactions are forwarded to an Amazon SQS queue, which asynchronously triggers a dedicated Lambda function to log and persist the records to Amazon S3. This separation of concerns provides SQS-backed durability, buffering, and independent scalability for the invalid-data handling path without affecting the fraud detection pipeline's performance. Keyword tip: SQS-triggered Lambda for asynchronous, decoupled processing of invalid or failed records is the canonical MLOps pattern when two processing paths have different latency and throughput requirements within the same SNS-triggered ML inference pipeline.
Use Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose to preprocess and validate the data stream, invoke AWS Lambda to forward valid transactions to SageMaker, and redirect invalid transactions directly to Amazon S3 via a Firehose delivery stream.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose is designed for high-throughput, continuous data streaming to destinations such as Amazon S3, Amazon Redshift, or Amazon OpenSearch Service — it does not natively perform schema validation against a predefined schema or conditionally route individual records to a SageMaker real-time endpoint based on per-record validation logic. Applying Firehose here mismatches the service's purpose with the scenario's requirement for per-record schema enforcement before ML inference. Additionally, adopting Firehose would require significant restructuring of the existing SNS-triggered Lambda architecture with no clear benefit over the SQS-based approach. Keyword tip: Kinesis Data Firehose is purpose-built for streaming delivery to storage and analytics destinations, not for per-record conditional routing to ML inference endpoints.
Use a single AWS Lambda function to validate the schema of each transaction, invoke SageMaker for fraud detection on valid data, and write invalid transactions directly to Amazon S3 within the same function execution.
Explanation
Incorrect. Performing all operations — schema validation, SageMaker endpoint invocation, and S3 writes for invalid data — within a single Lambda function introduces tight coupling and creates a performance risk under high transaction volumes. Writing invalid records to Amazon S3 synchronously within the same execution context as fraud detection can increase Lambda duration and introduce latency variability on the SageMaker inference path. This design also makes the two concerns — real-time fraud detection and invalid-record persistence — harder to scale, monitor, and tune independently. Keyword tip: Synchronous coupling of ML inference and logging within a single Lambda function degrades throughput; prefer asynchronous decoupling via an SQS queue to isolate the critical inference path.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests event-driven ML inference architecture and asynchronous decoupling.

It focuses on using Amazon SQS to separate invalid-record handling from the low-latency fraud detection path that invokes a Amazon SageMaker real-time endpoint.

It is tied to the Deployment and Orchestration of ML Workflows domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Set up an AWS Step Functions workflow to orchestrate schema validation, invoke AWS Lambda for processing valid transactions and sending them to SageMaker, and write invalid transactions to Amazon S3: This option is not the best answer. AWS Step Functions is useful for complex workflow orchestration, branching, retries, and long-running processes, but it adds unnecessary orchestration overhead for a simple per-record validation and routing pattern.

    Use AWS Lambda to validate the schema of each transaction, send valid data to SageMaker for real-time inference, and forward invalid data to an Amazon SQS queue, which triggers a separate Lambda function to log and store it in Amazon S3: This is the correct option. It keeps the critical inference path fast while moving invalid-record logging to an asynchronous, durable, independently scalable path.

    Use Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose to preprocess and validate the data stream, invoke AWS Lambda to forward valid transactions to SageMaker, and redirect invalid transactions directly to Amazon S3 via a Firehose delivery stream: This option is incorrect. Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose is optimized for delivering streaming data to destinations such as Amazon S3, Amazon Redshift, or Amazon OpenSearch Service. It is not the best fit for per-record schema validation and conditional routing to a real-time ML endpoint.

    Use a single AWS Lambda function to validate the schema of each transaction, invoke SageMaker for fraud detection on valid data, and write invalid transactions directly to Amazon S3 within the same function execution: This option is incorrect. Writing invalid records to Amazon S3 synchronously inside the same function can increase latency and reduce throughput on the fraud detection path.

Why Lambda with SQS decoupling fits this scenario.

The pipeline is event-driven.

The input comes through Amazon SNS.

The valid records must be sent to a Amazon SageMaker real-time endpoint for fraud detection.

The invalid records must still be preserved for logging, auditing, or later analysis.

These two paths have different latency requirements.

The valid-record path is latency-sensitive.

The invalid-record path is durability-sensitive but does not need to block real-time inference.

That points to asynchronous decoupling with Amazon SQS.

The first AWS Lambda function validates the schema.

If the record is valid, it invokes the Amazon SageMaker real-time endpoint.

If the record is invalid, it sends the record to an Amazon SQS queue.

A separate AWS Lambda function is then triggered by the queue and writes the invalid record to Amazon S3.

This design keeps the fraud detection path fast.

It also gives the invalid-record path buffering, retry handling, durability, and independent scaling.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Low-latency inference: Keep valid records on the direct path to Amazon SageMaker.

    Invalid-record durability: Store invalid events without blocking inference.

    Asynchronous decoupling: Use Amazon SQS.

    Independent scaling: Scale fraud detection and invalid-record persistence separately.

    Schema validation: Perform validation before invoking the ML endpoint.

    Separation of concerns: Keep inference logic separate from logging and persistence.

    Avoid synchronous side effects: Do not write invalid records to Amazon S3 in the same critical execution path.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

SNS-triggered inference + valid records to Amazon SageMaker + invalid records stored separately + low latency = Amazon SQS decoupling with separate AWS Lambda functions.

Remember:

    Amazon SNS: Event fanout and message notification service.

    AWS Lambda: Good for lightweight event-driven validation and endpoint invocation.

    Amazon SageMaker real-time endpoint: Used for low-latency inference.

    Amazon SQS: Buffers and decouples asynchronous work.

    Amazon S3: Durable storage for invalid records, logs, or audit data.

    AWS Step Functions: Better for complex orchestration, not simple decoupled routing.

    Amazon Kinesis Data Firehose: Delivery service, not per-record ML routing logic.

    Using AWS Lambda with Amazon SQS.

    Invoke an Amazon SageMaker real-time endpoint.

    Using AWS Lambda with Amazon SNS.

Question 49Skipped

A data science team at a large construction and engineering firm, BuildCore Solutions, is training a deep learning model that frequently encounters vanishing gradients and overfitting during the training process.


The team decides to integrate Amazon SageMaker Debugger to monitor and debug the model in real time, with the goal of resolving these issues before the model is promoted to a deployment pipeline. 


Which of the following actions should the team take when configuring SageMaker Debugger for this task? (Select TWO.)
Correct selection
Configure a Debugger hook in the training script to capture and log tensors at defined collection intervals, providing real-time insights into model weights and gradients.
Explanation
Correct. A SageMaker Debugger hook — configured via DebuggerHookConfig in the Estimator or through the SMDebug client library inside the training script — intercepts the training loop and saves tensor collections (weights, gradients, biases, and loss values) to Amazon S3 at user-defined step intervals. These serialized tensors are the raw data consumed by Debugger rules and by the SageMaker Studio Debugger dashboard, enabling real-time visibility into gradient behavior and weight updates throughout training. Keyword tip: 'Debugger hook,' 'tensor capture,' 'DebuggerHookConfig,' and 'tensor collections' are the defining identifiers of this SageMaker Debugger configuration step.
Enable SageMaker Model Monitor on the training job to visualize and track CPU and GPU resource utilization and flag data quality violations that may cause overfitting.
Explanation
Incorrect. SageMaker Model Monitor is a post-deployment service used to detect data quality drift, model quality drift, bias drift, and feature attribution drift on live inference endpoints. It does not operate on training jobs and has no capability to flag overfitting conditions or resource utilization anomalies during model training. Applying Model Monitor to a training job context is a common distractor on the MLA-C01 exam. Keyword tip: whenever 'Model Monitor' appears in the context of training-time issues such as vanishing gradients or overfitting, treat it as a distractor — Model Monitor operates exclusively on deployed inference endpoints.
Correct selection
Set up built-in or custom debug rules to monitor training metrics and automatically detect issues such as vanishing gradients or overfitting in real time.
Explanation
Correct. SageMaker Debugger provides a library of built-in rules — such as VanishingGradient, Overfit, and WeightUpdateRatio — that continuously evaluate tensor data emitted during training and fire alerts or stop the training job when anomalous conditions are detected. Teams can also author custom rules using the SMDebug SDK to address domain-specific diagnostic requirements. Enabling these rules is a core configuration step for real-time training anomaly detection. Keyword tip: 'built-in rules,' 'VanishingGradient rule,' and 'real-time anomaly detection during training' are strong signals that SageMaker Debugger rules are the correct tool.
Use SageMaker Experiments to register tensor snapshots as trial components, and configure automated stopping criteria based on gradient norms to prevent vanishing gradients.
Explanation
Incorrect. SageMaker Experiments is an experiment tracking and lineage service that logs metadata, metrics, parameters, and artifacts across training runs as trial components, enabling run comparison and reproducibility. While it integrates with SageMaker training jobs, it does not natively register tensor snapshots from Debugger nor provide automated stopping criteria based on gradient norms. Gradient-level monitoring and rule-based stopping actions are handled exclusively by SageMaker Debugger rules combined with training job stopping conditions. Keyword tip: SageMaker Experiments addresses run comparison and lineage tracking, not real-time tensor-level anomaly detection during training.
Use SageMaker Profiler to automatically detect and correct hyperparameters such as learning rate and batch size during training to mitigate vanishing gradients.
Explanation
Incorrect. SageMaker Profiler is designed to monitor system-level resource utilization — such as CPU, GPU, memory, and I/O throughput — and identify performance bottlenecks during training jobs. It does not automatically detect or correct hyperparameters such as learning rate or batch size; that responsibility belongs to SageMaker Automatic Model Tuning (Hyperparameter Optimization). Configuring Profiler would provide no mechanism for addressing vanishing gradients or overfitting at the tensor level. Keyword tip: when you see 'automatically correct hyperparameters during training,' recognize this as a distractor pointing to Automatic Model Tuning, not SageMaker Debugger or Profiler.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of Amazon SageMaker Debugger for training-time diagnostics.

It focuses on using Debugger hooks to capture tensors during training and Debugger rules to detect issues such as vanishing gradients, overfitting, or abnormal weight updates.

It is tied to the ML Model Development domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Use SageMaker Profiler to automatically detect and correct hyperparameters such as learning rate and batch size during training to mitigate vanishing gradients: This option is incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Profiler monitors system-level resource utilization, such as CPU, GPU, memory, and I/O. It does not automatically correct hyperparameters or analyze tensor-level issues such as vanishing gradients.

    Set up built-in or custom debug rules to monitor training metrics and automatically detect issues such as vanishing gradients or overfitting in real time: This is a correct option. Amazon SageMaker Debugger provides built-in rules such as VanishingGradient, Overfit, and WeightUpdateRatio. Custom rules can also be created for domain-specific training diagnostics.

    Enable SageMaker Model Monitor on the training job to visualize and track CPU and GPU resource utilization and flag data quality violations that may cause overfitting: This option is incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor is used after deployment to monitor live endpoints. It does not diagnose training-time tensor behavior or training job overfitting.

    Configure a Debugger hook in the training script to capture and log tensors at defined collection intervals, providing real-time insights into model weights and gradients: This is a correct option. A Debugger hook captures tensors such as weights, gradients, biases, activations, and losses during training and stores them for analysis.

    Use SageMaker Experiments to register tensor snapshots as trial components, and configure automated stopping criteria based on gradient norms to prevent vanishing gradients: This option is incorrect. Amazon SageMaker Experiments tracks runs, parameters, metrics, and artifacts, but it does not perform real-time tensor-level anomaly detection or gradient-based stopping.

Why Debugger rules and hooks fit this scenario.

The scenario describes training-time problems.

The team needs to detect issues such as vanishing gradients and overfitting.

Those issues require visibility into tensors, gradients, weights, and training behavior while the job is running.

That points directly to Amazon SageMaker Debugger.

A Debugger hook captures tensor data during training.

It can collect values such as gradients, weights, biases, activations, and losses at configured intervals.

Those tensors are written to Amazon S3.

Debugger rules then evaluate the captured tensors.

Built-in rules can detect common training issues such as VanishingGradient, Overfit, and abnormal WeightUpdateRatio.

Custom rules can also be created when the team needs specialized checks.

This makes Amazon SageMaker Debugger the correct service for real-time training anomaly detection.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Training-time diagnostics: Use Amazon SageMaker Debugger.

    Tensor capture: Configure a Debugger hook.

    Gradient monitoring: Capture and inspect gradients during training.

    Real-time anomaly detection: Use built-in or custom Debugger rules.

    Overfitting detection: Use rules such as Overfit.

    Vanishing gradient detection: Use the VanishingGradient rule.

    Avoid service confusion: Do not use Amazon SageMaker Model Monitor, Amazon SageMaker Experiments, or Amazon SageMaker Profiler for tensor-level training anomaly detection.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

    Amazon SageMaker Debugger: Captures and analyzes training tensors.
