Question 60Skipped

A media streaming company, StreamVault Media, operates a large fleet of EC2 instances that support its ML training and inference workloads  including real-time recommendation engines and content classification models.


The operations team requires continuous monitoring of both CPU utilization and disk read/write operations across all instances.


Additionally, the compliance team mandates that all configuration changes to these EC2 instances including modifications to security groups, instance types, and AMI IDs  must be tracked and auditable over time.


Which combination of AWS services best satisfies both the performance monitoring and configuration tracking requirements?
Enable AWS CloudTrail to monitor CPU and disk usage metrics, and use AWS Config to capture configuration state changes.
Explanation
Incorrect. AWS CloudTrail records AWS API calls (e.g., RunInstances, ModifyInstanceAttribute) but does not collect or report operating-system-level performance metrics such as CPU utilization or disk I/O. Those metrics require Amazon CloudWatch, typically with the CloudWatch Agent installed on the instance. Furthermore, CloudTrail does not maintain a continuous, structured configuration history in the queryable format that AWS Config provides for compliance evaluation. Keyword tip: Never substitute CloudTrail for a metrics collection tool; it is an API-level audit service only and cannot replace CloudWatch for operational performance data.
Configure Amazon CloudWatch Alarms for CPU and disk operations, and enable AWS CloudTrail to track configuration changes on the EC2 instances.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon CloudWatch Alarms are appropriate for threshold-based alerting on CPU and disk metrics; however, AWS CloudTrail is designed to record AWS API call history — capturing who performed which API action and when — not to maintain a structured, queryable history of resource configuration states over time, such as changes to security group associations or instance type attributes. For configuration tracking and compliance auditing of EC2 resource attributes, AWS Config is the correct service. Keyword tip: When a question pairs performance metrics with compliance-grade configuration history, CloudTrail is a distractor — AWS Config owns the configuration timeline.
Use Amazon CloudWatch Logs to capture both CPU utilization data and configuration changes, and create custom metric filters for disk read/write operations.
Explanation
Incorrect. Amazon CloudWatch Logs is designed to ingest, store, and query log streams emitted by applications, services, and the CloudWatch Agent — not to natively surface OS-level performance metrics or structured resource configuration changes. Attempting to derive CPU utilization and disk I/O metrics solely through log metric filters adds significant operational complexity and does not produce the structured configuration tracking with change history that compliance requirements demand. Keyword tip: CloudWatch Logs plus metric filters can extract custom metrics from log data, but this approach is unsuitable as a replacement for native CloudWatch Metrics and AWS Config in compliance-driven infrastructure monitoring scenarios.
Correct answer
Use Amazon CloudWatch to monitor CPU utilization and disk read/write operations, and use AWS Config to track configuration changes to the EC2 instances.
Explanation
Correct. Amazon CloudWatch natively collects EC2 performance metrics — including CPUUtilization, DiskReadOps, DiskWriteOps, DiskReadBytes, and DiskWriteBytes — enabling real-time monitoring, alarming, and dashboarding on instance health without additional tooling. AWS Config continuously records the configuration state of EC2 instances and evaluates changes to attributes such as security group associations, instance type, and AMI ID, providing a full, queryable configuration timeline that satisfies compliance audit requirements. This combination directly and precisely addresses both the operational performance monitoring and the configuration governance requirements described in the scenario. Keyword tip: CloudWatch Metrics equals operational performance data; AWS Config equals configuration timeline, change history, and compliance rules for AWS resources — recognize this pairing whenever a question combines infrastructure observability with auditability.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests the ability to select the correct AWS services for two separate monitoring requirements:

    Real-time infrastructure performance monitoring.

    Configuration change tracking for compliance.

It focuses on using Amazon CloudWatch for operational metrics and AWS Config for configuration history, compliance evaluation, and auditability.

It is tied to the ML Solution Monitoring, Maintenance, and Security domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Configure Amazon CloudWatch Alarms for CPU and disk operations, and enable AWS CloudTrail to track configuration changes on the EC2 instances: This option is incorrect. Amazon CloudWatch Alarms are appropriate for CPU and disk metric alerts, but AWS CloudTrail records API activity. It does not maintain a structured configuration state timeline for compliance. Use AWS Config for configuration tracking.

    Enable AWS CloudTrail to monitor CPU and disk usage metrics, and use AWS Config to capture configuration state changes: This option is incorrect. AWS CloudTrail does not collect CPU utilization or disk I/O metrics. Those are operational metrics handled by Amazon CloudWatch. AWS Config is correctly associated with configuration state changes, but the CloudTrail portion is wrong.

    Use Amazon CloudWatch Logs to capture both CPU utilization data and configuration changes, and create custom metric filters for disk read/write operations: This option is incorrect. Amazon CloudWatch Logs stores and analyzes log events. Metric filters can extract custom metrics from logs, but this is not the native or lowest-overhead approach for EC2 performance metrics. It also does not replace AWS Config for structured configuration history and compliance evaluation.

    Use Amazon CloudWatch to monitor CPU utilization and disk read/write operations, and use AWS Config to track configuration changes to the EC2 instances: This is the correct option. Amazon CloudWatch monitors EC2 performance metrics such as CPUUtilization, DiskReadOps, DiskWriteOps, DiskReadBytes, and DiskWriteBytes. AWS Config records resource configuration changes and provides a compliance-oriented configuration timeline.

Why CloudWatch and AWS Config fit this scenario.

The scenario has two different requirements.

The first requirement is operational monitoring.

The team needs visibility into CPU utilization and disk read/write activity.

That points to Amazon CloudWatch.

Amazon CloudWatch collects metrics from AWS resources and supports dashboards, alarms, anomaly detection, and automated notifications.

For Amazon EC2, important metrics include CPUUtilization, DiskReadOps, DiskWriteOps, DiskReadBytes, and DiskWriteBytes.

The second requirement is compliance tracking.

The team needs to know when EC2 configuration changes occur.

That points to AWS Config.

AWS Config records resource configuration history over time.

It can track changes such as instance attributes, security group associations, AMI IDs, and other configuration state changes.

It can also evaluate resources against compliance rules.

AWS CloudTrail is still useful, but it solves a different problem.

It records API activity, such as who called an API and when.

It does not replace AWS Config for configuration state history.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Performance monitoring: Use Amazon CloudWatch.

    CPU and disk metrics: Monitor EC2 operational metrics.

    Threshold-based alerting: Use CloudWatch Alarms.

    Configuration history: Use AWS Config.

    Compliance evaluation: Use AWS Config rules and resource timelines.

    Avoid audit-tool confusion: Do not use AWS CloudTrail as a metrics or configuration-state service.

Question 61Skipped

A data scientist at PixelForge Games, an online gaming company, is facing the challenge of high variance in a machine learning model used to predict player churn, where the model performs well on training data but poorly on new, unseen player data.


The team must address this issue while adhering to responsible AI principles, which include transparency, reproducibility, and robust evaluation practices.


Which of the following methods would MOST LIKELY improve the model's ability to generalize?
Increase the number of parameters in the model and add additional hidden layers to capture more complex patterns present in the training data.
Explanation
Incorrect. Increasing model complexity — by adding more parameters or additional hidden layers — directly worsens the high-variance problem rather than alleviating it. A more expressive model is more likely to memorize noise in the training data instead of learning generalizable patterns, which is the root cause of overfitting. Strategies that increase capacity are appropriate when the model is underfitting (high bias), not when it is already overfitting (high variance). Keyword tip: when the scenario describes high variance or a model that memorizes training data, increasing model capacity is a classic trap distractor on the MLA-C01 exam.
Focus on training the model using a smaller, more curated subset of the training data to reduce the influence of noisy samples and prevent overfitting.
Explanation
Incorrect. Training on a smaller subset of data typically increases the risk of underfitting, where the model fails to capture important patterns due to insufficient exposure to the full data distribution. While data curation can reduce label noise, arbitrarily shrinking the training set does not address high variance and may shift the problem toward high bias, introducing a different bias-variance imbalance. Keyword tip: reducing training data size as a remedy for overfitting is a commonly tested incorrect approach in MLA-C01 scenarios that describe high variance or poor generalization on unseen data.
Remove cross-validation steps to speed up model training and testing, then re-evaluate the model solely on the training set to confirm performance improvements.
Explanation
Incorrect. Removing cross-validation undermines the model's ability to generalize by eliminating a key mechanism for estimating out-of-sample performance. Cross-validation is a responsible AI practice that promotes robust and reproducible evaluation; discarding it contradicts the explicit constraints stated in the scenario. Re-evaluating solely on the training set would produce an overly optimistic and misleading performance estimate, which is the opposite of addressing high variance. Keyword tip: when a scenario explicitly mentions responsible AI principles and generalization, removing evaluation safeguards such as cross-validation is always a distractor on the MLA-C01 exam.
Correct answer
Use early stopping to halt the training process once the model's performance starts to degrade on the validation set, preventing the model from overfitting the training data.
Explanation
Correct. Early stopping is a well-established regularization technique that monitors the model's performance on a held-out validation set during training. When validation performance begins to degrade while training loss continues to decrease — a hallmark of overfitting — early stopping halts the training process at the optimal checkpoint, effectively balancing the bias-variance tradeoff. This approach directly addresses high variance, preserves responsible AI practices by maintaining a proper validation set, and avoids unnecessary compute costs. Keyword tip: high variance combined with degrading validation set performance → early stopping is the canonical MLA-C01 answer for this pattern, applicable in both SageMaker training jobs and custom training loops.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests understanding of the bias-variance tradeoff and regularization techniques.

It focuses on recognizing high variance as overfitting and selecting early stopping as an effective technique to stop training when validation performance begins to degrade.

It is tied to the ML Model Development domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Remove cross-validation steps to speed up model training and testing, then re-evaluate the model solely on the training set to confirm performance improvements: This option is incorrect. Removing cross-validation weakens evaluation quality and gives a misleading view of model performance. Evaluating only on the training set hides overfitting instead of solving it.

    Focus on training the model using a smaller, more curated subset of the training data to reduce the influence of noisy samples and prevent overfitting: This option is incorrect. Reducing the training dataset can remove useful signal and increase the risk of underfitting. It does not directly solve high variance.

    Increase the number of parameters in the model and add additional hidden layers to capture more complex patterns present in the training data: This option is incorrect. Increasing model complexity usually worsens high variance. A larger model can memorize the training data more easily, which increases overfitting risk.

    Use early stopping to halt the training process once the model's performance starts to degrade on the validation set, preventing the model from overfitting the training data: This is the correct option. Early stopping monitors validation performance during training and stops the process when generalization begins to degrade.

Why early stopping fits this scenario.

The model has high variance.

That means it is likely overfitting the training data.

The model may continue improving on the training set while getting worse on the validation set.

That pattern means the model is learning noise or training-specific details instead of generalizable patterns.

Early stopping addresses this directly.

It monitors a validation metric during training.

When validation performance stops improving or begins to degrade, training is stopped.

This preserves the model checkpoint that generalizes best to unseen data.

Early stopping also avoids unnecessary compute cost because it prevents the model from continuing training after the useful learning phase has passed.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    High variance: Use a regularization technique.

    Overfitting risk: Stop before the model memorizes training noise.

    Validation-aware training: Monitor held-out validation performance.

    Responsible evaluation: Keep validation or cross-validation safeguards.

    No architecture change required: Avoid increasing model complexity.

    Compute efficiency: Avoid unnecessary training epochs.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

High variance + validation performance degrades during training = early stopping.

Remember:

    High variance: Strong signal for overfitting.

    Overfitting: Training performance improves while validation/test performance worsens.

    Early stopping: Stops training at the best validation checkpoint.

    Validation set: Required to detect generalization degradation.

    Cross-validation: Helps estimate out-of-sample performance.

    Increasing model complexity: Usually worsens overfitting.

    Reducing data size: Usually hurts generalization.

Question 62Skipped

A data engineer at PixelArena Games, an online gaming company, is tasked with building an Extract, Transform, Load (ETL) pipeline using AWS Glue to process operational player engagement and in-game transaction data from various upstream sources.


The raw data, stored in CSV format in Amazon S3, must be converted into a columnar format to enable faster analytical querying through Amazon Athena. Additionally, the solution must ensure that the schema of each data source is automatically detected and persisted for future pipeline executions without manual intervention. 


Which of the following steps should the data engineer take to meet these requirements? (Select TWO.)


Correct selection
Use an AWS Glue crawler to automatically detect the schema of the CSV files in Amazon S3 and store the resulting metadata in the AWS Glue Data Catalog.
Explanation
Correct. AWS Glue crawlers are specifically designed to connect to data stores such as Amazon S3, infer schema from the underlying data files, and automatically populate the AWS Glue Data Catalog with table definitions and partition information. This automated discovery process eliminates manual schema management and ensures that schema changes in source data are captured in subsequent scheduled crawler runs. The resulting catalog entries are immediately available for querying by Amazon Athena and for use by downstream AWS Glue ETL jobs, satisfying both the automation and persistence requirements in this scenario. Keyword tip: 'automatically detect schema' combined with 'persist metadata for future executions' in a Glue ETL scenario is the definitive signal for an AWS Glue crawler writing to the AWS Glue Data Catalog.
Deploy an AWS Lambda function triggered by S3 events to process the CSV files, infer their schema dynamically, and register the inferred schema in Amazon Athena for storage.
Explanation
Incorrect. While AWS Lambda can be triggered by Amazon S3 events and suits lightweight, event-driven tasks, it is not designed for large-scale ETL workloads, schema inference across heterogeneous data sources, or native integration with the AWS Glue Data Catalog for persistent metadata storage. Lambda functions enforcing custom schema inference logic would require substantial development effort, lack the catalog integration that Glue provides out of the box, and face execution duration limits that make them unsuitable for processing large volumes of PixelArena Games' transaction data. Keyword tip: Lambda is a distractor in ETL and automated schema management scenarios where AWS Glue provides a fully managed, purpose-built alternative — watch for options that introduce custom code complexity to solve problems Glue handles natively.
Correct selection
Use AWS Glue's built-in transformation capability to convert the CSV data into Apache Parquet format before writing the output back to Amazon S3.
Explanation
Correct. AWS Glue natively supports format conversion transformations, including converting delimited text formats such as CSV into Apache Parquet. Parquet is a columnar storage format that dramatically improves query performance and reduces data scan costs in Amazon Athena by allowing predicate pushdown and column pruning. Using AWS Glue's built-in DynamicFrame write operations with the format parameter set to 'parquet' fulfills the columnar format requirement without additional custom tooling or external services. Keyword tip: CSV-to-Parquet conversion for Athena query performance optimization is a canonical AWS Glue ETL use case on the MLA-C01 exam — pair 'columnar format' with 'Glue built-in transformation' as a reliable signal.
Manually create table schemas directly in Amazon Athena and then upload the schema definitions to the AWS Glue Data Catalog to enable downstream querying.
Explanation
Incorrect. Manually creating schemas in Amazon Athena and then pushing them to the AWS Glue Data Catalog is an inverted and inefficient workflow that introduces ongoing manual maintenance as PixelArena Games' data sources evolve. The recommended pattern is for the AWS Glue crawler to populate the Data Catalog automatically, after which Athena can query the resulting table definitions directly — not the reverse. This approach also cannot satisfy the requirement for automatic schema detection without manual intervention. Keyword tip: any option that proposes manual schema creation in Athena as a precursor to Glue Data Catalog population is a distractor in automated data preparation scenarios on the MLA-C01 exam.
Write custom scripts to manually define the schema for each data source and register those schema definitions in Amazon RDS for centralized storage.
Explanation
Incorrect. Manually writing schema definition scripts and storing them in Amazon RDS introduces unnecessary operational overhead and defeats the purpose of using AWS Glue's managed automation capabilities. Amazon RDS is a relational database service designed for transactional workloads, not for serving as a schema catalog in a Glue-based ETL pipeline; the AWS Glue Data Catalog is the purpose-built metadata store for this role. This approach also requires continuous manual updates whenever PixelArena Games adds new data sources or modifies existing schemas. Keyword tip: when a question requires automatic schema detection in a Glue ETL pipeline, manual scripting combined with RDS-based storage is a clear distractor signaling high operational overhead and architectural mismatch.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

It focuses on using AWS Glue crawlers for automated schema discovery and AWS Glue ETL jobs to convert CSV data into Apache Parquet for better analytics performance.

It is tied to the Data Preparation for Machine Learning (ML) domain.

    Use AWS Glue's built-in transformation capability to convert the CSV data into Apache Parquet format before writing the output back to Amazon S3: This is a correct option. AWS Glue can transform CSV data into Apache Parquet, a columnar format that improves query performance and reduces scanned data for services such as Amazon Athena.

    Manually create table schemas directly in Amazon Athena and then upload the schema definitions to the AWS Glue Data Catalog to enable downstream querying: This option is incorrect. The workflow is backwards. In the recommended pattern, an AWS Glue crawler discovers schemas and populates the AWS Glue Data Catalog, then Amazon Athena queries those cataloged tables.

    Use an AWS Glue crawler to automatically detect the schema of the CSV files in Amazon S3 and store the resulting metadata in the AWS Glue Data Catalog: This is a correct option. AWS Glue crawlers can inspect data in Amazon S3, infer schemas, detect partitions, and register metadata in the AWS Glue Data Catalog.

    Deploy an AWS Lambda function triggered by S3 events to process the CSV files, infer their schema dynamically, and register the inferred schema in Amazon Athena for storage: This option is incorrect. AWS Lambda can handle lightweight event-driven tasks, but it is not the best tool for scalable ETL schema discovery and catalog management. AWS Glue provides this capability natively.

Why Glue crawlers and CSV-to-Parquet conversion fit this scenario.

The data is stored in Amazon S3.

The source format is CSV.

The team needs automated schema detection.

The team also needs an optimized format for downstream analytics and ML workflows.

That points to AWS Glue crawlers and AWS Glue ETL jobs.

An AWS Glue crawler can scan the CSV files in Amazon S3.

It infers the schema and registers table metadata in the AWS Glue Data Catalog.

This eliminates manual schema management.

Apache Parquet is a columnar format.

It improves query performance because engines can read only the required columns instead of scanning full rows.

It also reduces storage and scan costs through efficient compression and column pruning.

    Athena performance: Reduce bytes scanned by Amazon Athena.

    Managed ETL: Avoid custom schema scripts or AWS Lambda-based ETL logic.

Question 63Skipped

A mining and minerals company, OreVault Extractives, is using Amazon SageMaker to train a reinforcement learning agent to navigate autonomous haul trucks through a dynamic open-pit mine environment.


During training, the agent consistently moves farther away from the designated loading zone rather than closer to it, and this behavior persists across multiple training iterations. 


What is the most likely root cause of this behavior? 
Correct answer
Positive rewards that are incorrectly assigned when the agent moves away from the loading zone, reinforcing undesirable behavior
Explanation
Correct. In reinforcement learning, an agent's behavior is shaped almost entirely by the reward signal it receives. If the agent consistently moves farther from the loading zone across multiple training iterations, the most likely explanation is that the reward function is flawed and is issuing positive rewards when the agent moves in the wrong direction. This incorrect positive reinforcement actively teaches the agent that moving away from the goal is beneficial, causing it to converge on a systematically incorrect policy. Reward function design is one of the most critical and error-prone aspects of reinforcement learning engineering on Amazon SageMaker. Keyword tip: when an RL agent trained on SageMaker converges persistently on the wrong behavior, always inspect the reward function for incorrect positive signal assignment before investigating the policy architecture, action space, or exploration strategy.
A suboptimal policy function that selects actions based on insufficiently converged value estimates
Explanation
Incorrect. The policy function maps observed states to actions and is learned as a direct consequence of the reward signal the agent receives. If the agent persistently moves away from the loading zone, the policy has converged to a wrong behavior — but this is a symptom, not the root cause. The underlying cause is the flawed reward feedback that shaped the policy in the first place; a correctly specified reward function would drive the policy toward the intended behavior regardless of initialization. Keyword tip: policy-level issues are typically framed as slow convergence, instability, or exploration inefficiency — not persistent and systematic convergence toward the wrong objective.
Incorrectly specified action space that expands the set of available movements for the haul truck agent
Explanation
Incorrect. The action space defines the set of possible actions available to the agent — for example, accelerate, decelerate, turn left, or stop. Misspecifying or expanding the action space changes what the agent can do, but it does not directly cause the agent to be rewarded for moving in the wrong direction. Even with an altered action space, the agent would still converge toward high-reward behaviors if the reward signal were correctly designed. Keyword tip: action space misspecification manifests as infeasible, missing, or redundant actions — not as systematic and persistent misdirection toward an incorrect goal.
Negative rewards or penalties that are either absent, too weak, or inconsistently applied for moving away from the loading zone
Explanation
Incorrect. Negative rewards and penalties are designed to discourage undesirable actions such as moving away from the loading zone. If penalties were correctly implemented, the agent would learn to avoid those penalized behaviors over time. The scenario describes persistent incorrect movement across multiple iterations, which implies the agent is being actively reinforced for this behavior — not merely failing to be deterred from it. Absent or weak penalties contribute to slow or noisy learning, but the most direct and complete explanation for strong convergent misdirection is the presence of incorrect positive reinforcement. Keyword tip: weak or missing penalties cause slow learning or undirected exploration bias; they do not by themselves produce strong, consistent convergence toward the wrong goal.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests understanding of the reward function in reinforcement learning.

It focuses on identifying that persistent incorrect behavior in an RL agent is usually caused by a flawed reward signal, especially when positive rewards are assigned to undesirable actions.

It is tied to the ML Model Development domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Incorrectly specified action space that expands the set of available movements for the haul truck agent: This option is incorrect. The action space defines what actions the agent can take, such as move forward, turn, stop, or reverse. An expanded action space may make training harder, but it does not directly explain why the agent consistently learns to move away from the target.

    A suboptimal policy function that selects actions based on insufficiently converged value estimates: This option is not the best answer. A poor policy can produce bad actions, but the policy is learned from the reward signal. If the agent repeatedly converges toward the wrong behavior, the deeper issue is likely the reward function that trained the policy.

    Negative rewards or penalties that are either absent, too weak, or inconsistently applied for moving away from the loading zone: This option is plausible but not the strongest answer. Weak or missing penalties can make learning slower or less directed. However, persistent movement away from the target suggests the agent is being actively rewarded for the wrong behavior.

    Positive rewards that are incorrectly assigned when the agent moves away from the loading zone, reinforcing undesirable behavior: This is the correct option. In reinforcement learning, the agent learns from rewards. If moving away from the loading zone receives a positive reward, the agent will learn that this behavior is desirable and continue doing it.

Why incorrect positive rewards fit this scenario.

The scenario describes a reinforcement learning agent.

The agent repeatedly moves away from the target loading zone.

This is not random exploration.

It is persistent and systematic behavior.

That points to a reward function problem.

In reinforcement learning, the reward function defines what the agent should optimize.

If the reward function is wrong, the agent can learn the wrong behavior very effectively.

If moving away from the loading zone is incorrectly rewarded, the agent receives positive feedback for undesirable behavior.

Over many training iterations, that reward signal shapes the policy.

The policy then converges toward the behavior that maximizes reward, even though the behavior is wrong from the business perspective.

This is why reward design is one of the most important parts of reinforcement learning.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Persistent wrong behavior: Inspect the reward function.

    Systematic convergence: Look for incorrect reinforcement, not random noise.

    Wrong objective: Positive reward may be assigned to the wrong action.

    Policy behavior: Understand that the policy is shaped by rewards.

    RL debugging: Validate reward signals before blaming the model architecture.

    SageMaker RL workflows: Treat reward shaping as a core design concern.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

RL agent consistently learns the wrong behavior = inspect the reward function for incorrect positive rewards.

Remember:

    Reward function: Defines what behavior the agent learns to optimize.

    Positive reward: Reinforces behavior.

    Incorrect positive reward: Teaches the agent the wrong objective.

    Policy: Learned behavior produced from reward-driven training.

    Action space: Defines possible actions, but not what is desirable.

    Weak penalties: May slow learning, but do not explain strong positive convergence as well as wrong rewards.

    Reward shaping: Critical reinforcement learning design task.

Official AWS references.

    Train reinforcement learning models in Amazon SageMaker.

Key exam terms: reinforcement learning, reward function, positive reward misassignment, policy convergence, action space, Amazon SageMaker, reward shaping, systematic misbehavior.

Question 64Skipped

A hospitality and hotel chain company, StayBridge Hotels, is designing its AWS infrastructure security model and needs to delegate permissions across multiple services and accounts.


A cloud architect is reviewing how AWS IAM roles should be configured for their ML platform, which uses Amazon SageMaker for demand forecasting and AWS Lambda for real-time pricing automation.


Which of the following statements about AWS IAM roles is correct?
IAM roles are exclusively used by AWS services such as Amazon SageMaker or AWS Lambda and cannot be assumed by IAM users, federated identities, or external accounts.
Explanation
Incorrect. While AWS services are a very common consumer of IAM roles (e.g., a SageMaker execution role), roles are not limited to AWS services. IAM users in the same account, IAM users in other AWS accounts (cross-account access), federated identities through SAML 2.0 or OIDC, and web identity principals (e.g., Amazon Cognito) can all assume roles. Restricting roles to AWS services only would describe a specific trust policy configuration, not the inherent capability of IAM roles in general. Keyword tip: If an option uses the word 'exclusively' to limit IAM role usage to a single type of principal, treat it as a red flag — IAM roles support a broad set of trust relationships by design.
Correct answer
IAM roles can be assumed by AWS services or external identities and can have multiple policies attached to manage permissions.
Explanation
Correct. IAM roles are identity constructs that can be assumed by AWS services (e.g., Amazon SageMaker training jobs, AWS Lambda functions), IAM users within the same or a different account, federated users via SAML 2.0 or OIDC, and web identity providers. A single role can have multiple managed policies and inline policies attached simultaneously, enabling fine-grained, composable permission management. In MLA-C01 scenarios, SageMaker execution roles are IAM roles assumed by the SageMaker service on behalf of your workloads — they are not tied to any individual user. Keyword tip: When a question describes cross-account or cross-service ML access patterns (e.g., SageMaker accessing Amazon S3 or AWS Glue), IAM roles with trust policies are the correct delegation mechanism.
IAM roles are associated directly with a single IAM user at creation time and remain permanently bound to that specific user account for their entire lifecycle.
Explanation
Incorrect. IAM roles are not bound to any specific IAM user. Unlike IAM users, which have permanent long-term credentials, roles provide temporary security credentials through AWS STS (AssumeRole). Any principal — a user, an AWS service, or an external federated identity — that has the appropriate trust policy permission can assume a role. Confusing roles with users is a common distractor in security questions; the key distinguishing trait of a role is its lack of permanent association with a single identity. Keyword tip: Whenever an option claims a role is 'permanently bound' to a user or identity, recognize that as incorrect — IAM roles always issue temporary credentials via AWS STS, never long-term ones.
IAM roles do not support inline policies; all permission boundaries must be defined exclusively through AWS managed policies or customer managed policies stored in the IAM policy library.
Explanation
Incorrect. IAM roles fully support both managed policies (AWS managed and customer managed) and inline policies. Inline policies are embedded directly in the role and are deleted when the role is deleted, whereas managed policies are standalone objects that can be attached to multiple identities. Best practice generally favors managed policies for reusability and auditing, but inline policies remain a valid and supported option when a one-to-one permission boundary is desired. Keyword tip: When an option claims that inline policies are 'unsupported' or that only managed policies are allowed, recognize this as factually incorrect — both policy types are valid for IAM roles, users, and groups.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests foundational knowledge of AWS IAM roles and how they are used to secure ML workloads on AWS.

It focuses on understanding that IAM roles can be assumed by AWS services, IAM users, federated identities, and external accounts, and that roles can have multiple policies attached to define permissions.

It is tied to the ML Solution Monitoring, Maintenance, and Security domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    IAM roles can be assumed by AWS services or external identities and can have multiple policies attached to manage permissions: This is the correct option. IAM roles can be assumed by AWS services such as Amazon SageMaker, AWS Lambda, and AWS Glue, as well as by IAM users, federated identities, web identities, or principals from other AWS accounts. Roles can also have multiple managed and inline policies attached.

    IAM roles are associated directly with a single IAM user at creation time and remain permanently bound to that specific user account for their entire lifecycle: This option is incorrect. IAM roles are not permanently tied to one user. They are assumed by trusted principals and provide temporary credentials through AWS STS.

    IAM roles are exclusively used by AWS services such as Amazon SageMaker or AWS Lambda and cannot be assumed by IAM users, federated identities, or external accounts: This option is incorrect. AWS services commonly assume roles, but roles are not exclusive to services. Users, federated identities, web identities, and cross-account principals can also assume roles when the trust policy allows it.

    IAM roles do not support inline policies; all permission boundaries must be defined exclusively through AWS managed policies or customer managed policies stored in the IAM policy library: This option is incorrect. IAM roles support both managed policies and inline policies. Managed policies are reusable, while inline policies are embedded directly into a specific role.

Why IAM roles fit this scenario.

The scenario involves secure access for ML workloads.

Services such as Amazon SageMaker, AWS Glue, and AWS Lambda often need permissions to access resources like Amazon S3, Amazon CloudWatch, AWS KMS, or Amazon ECR.

The correct way to grant those permissions is through IAM roles.

An IAM role has two important parts.

The first is the trust policy.

The trust policy defines who can assume the role.

For example, it can allow Amazon SageMaker to assume a SageMaker execution role.

The second is the permission policy.

Permission policies define what the role is allowed to do after it is assumed.

For example, a SageMaker execution role might allow s3:GetObject, s3:PutObject, kms:Decrypt, or logs:CreateLogStream.

This avoids embedding long-term credentials in notebooks, scripts, containers, or pipeline code.

It also supports least-privilege access and cross-account delegation.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Temporary credentials: Use AWS STS role assumption.

    Service access: Allow AWS services such as Amazon SageMaker to assume execution roles.

    Cross-account delegation: Allow trusted principals from other accounts when needed.

    Least privilege: Attach only the permissions required for the workload.

    Flexible policy model: Use managed policies or inline policies.

    Secure MLOps pattern: Avoid long-term user credentials in ML workloads.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

Temporary delegated access + AWS service execution identity + least privilege = IAM role.

Remember:

    IAM role: Identity that can be assumed to obtain temporary credentials.

    Trust policy: Defines who can assume the role.

    Permission policy: Defines what the role can do.

    AWS STS AssumeRole: Issues temporary credentials for the role.

    Amazon SageMaker execution role: Role assumed by SageMaker workloads.

    Managed policies: Reusable policies attachable to multiple identities.

    Inline policies: Policies embedded directly in a single role.

Question 65Skipped

A logistics and freight company, FreightPath Logistics, uses AWS Glue to automate their ETL processes for shipment tracking and cargo data stored in various formats.


They are planning to optimize the performance of their AWS Glue ETL jobs, which currently handle large volumes of Parquet files ingested incrementally on a recurring schedule.


The team needs the most cost-effective approach to optimize these jobs without compromising throughput or data completeness.
Enable worker auto-scaling and increase the number of Data Processing Units (DPUs) allocated to each ETL job to maximize parallelism.
Explanation
Incorrect. While increasing DPUs can reduce wall-clock job duration by adding parallelism, it proportionally increases the per-run compute cost because AWS Glue billing is based on DPU-hours consumed. For incremental workloads where only a subset of shipment records changes between runs, adding DPUs does not address the underlying inefficiency of re-scanning unchanged data — it merely processes that redundant work faster and at greater expense. Job bookmarking eliminates the redundant work entirely, making it a fundamentally superior cost-optimization strategy in this context. Keyword tip: increasing DPUs optimizes latency at the direct expense of cost; job bookmarking optimizes cost by reducing the total amount of work performed per run.
Correct answer
Implement job bookmarking to track data processed between job runs and reduce redundant data processing.
Explanation
Correct. AWS Glue job bookmarking persists the state of a job run so that subsequent runs process only new or changed data rather than re-scanning the entire dataset. For incremental Parquet workloads such as FreightPath Logistics's recurring shipment ingestion, this directly reduces the volume of data read and the compute time billed per run, delivering both cost savings and sustained throughput without risking data completeness. Keyword tip: whenever a scenario mentions incremental data ingestion, recurring schedules, or avoiding reprocessing in AWS Glue, job bookmarking is the canonical cost-optimization mechanism to identify.
Convert all Parquet files into CSV format to simplify the processing logic within the ETL jobs.
Explanation
Incorrect. Parquet is a columnar, compressed binary format specifically designed for analytical query efficiency and reduced I/O. Converting Parquet files to CSV produces larger, uncompressed, row-oriented files that require more Amazon S3 storage, more I/O bandwidth, and longer processing time — all of which increase cost and degrade performance rather than optimize it. Keyword tip: if a question involves converting from an efficient columnar format such as Parquet or ORC to a row-based format such as CSV, this is almost always a performance and cost anti-pattern in AWS data engineering scenarios.
Manually trigger ETL jobs instead of scheduling them to exercise precise control over when compute resources are utilized.
Explanation
Incorrect. Replacing scheduled triggers with manual triggers removes the automation that enables consistent, predictable resource usage across recurring freight data ingestion pipelines. In practice, manual invocation introduces operational overhead, risks missed processing windows, and does not reduce the volume of data processed per run — which is the root driver of cost in this scenario. Keyword tip: manual triggering is an operational anti-pattern for high-volume, recurring ETL workloads; it is never the recommended cost-optimization technique in AWS Glue.
Overall explanation

What this question tests.

This question tests knowledge of AWS Glue cost optimization for incremental ETL workloads.

It focuses on using job bookmarking to avoid reprocessing the same data across recurring job runs.

It is tied to the Data Preparation for Machine Learning (ML) domain.

The concepts at a glance.

    Implement job bookmarking to track data processed between job runs and reduce redundant data processing: This is the correct option. AWS Glue job bookmarking tracks previously processed data and allows later job runs to process only new or changed records. This reduces redundant reads, processing time, and compute cost.

    Manually trigger ETL jobs instead of scheduling them to exercise precise control over when compute resources are utilized: This option is incorrect. Manual triggering does not reduce the amount of data processed. It also adds operational overhead and increases the risk of missed or inconsistent ETL runs.

    Convert all Parquet files into CSV format to simplify the processing logic within the ETL jobs: This option is incorrect. Parquet is a compressed columnar format optimized for analytics and efficient processing. Converting it to CSV usually increases storage size, scan volume, and processing time.

    Enable worker auto-scaling and increase the number of Data Processing Units allocated to each ETL job to maximize parallelism: This option is incorrect for cost optimization. Increasing DPUs may reduce runtime, but it can increase compute cost. It does not solve the root issue of reprocessing unchanged data.

Why AWS Glue job bookmarking fits this scenario.

The ETL workload runs repeatedly.

Only part of the dataset changes between runs.

Reprocessing the full dataset every time wastes compute.

That points directly to AWS Glue job bookmarking.

Job bookmarking stores state from previous job runs.

On the next run, AWS Glue can use that state to identify which data has already been processed.

The job can then process only new or modified data.

This reduces the amount of data read.

It also reduces job duration and DPU-hour consumption.

This is especially valuable for recurring ingestion pipelines that process formats such as Parquet, where the data is already optimized for analytical workloads.

This satisfies the key requirements:

    Incremental ETL: Use AWS Glue job bookmarking.

    Avoid redundant processing: Skip data that was already processed.

    Reduce compute cost: Lower total DPU-hour usage.

    Preserve data completeness: Continue processing new or changed data.

    Keep efficient file format: Retain Parquet instead of converting to CSV.

    Avoid manual operations: Do not replace scheduled automation with manual triggers.

Exam tip.

The key exam signal is:

AWS Glue + recurring ETL + incremental data + avoid reprocessing = job bookmarking.

Remember:

    AWS Glue job bookmarking: Tracks processed data between job runs.

    Incremental ETL: Processes only new or changed records.

    DPU-hours: Main compute billing dimension for AWS Glue jobs.

    Parquet: Efficient columnar format for analytics and ETL.

    CSV: Usually less efficient for large analytical pipelines.

    More DPUs: Can improve speed, but does not automatically reduce cost.

    Manual triggers: Operational workaround, not a cost optimization strategy.

Official AWS references.

    Tracking processed data using job bookmarks in AWS Glue.

Key exam terms: AWS Glue, job bookmarking, incremental ETL, DPU-hours, Parquet, columnar format, cost optimization, recurring schedule.
